class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans + 1

Reason in a repair-and-recheck way.

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Let's think about LeetCode "Longest WPI" constraints and known solution. Many solutions use prefix sum and map. Standard:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, hours: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(hours)
        ans = 0
        score = 0
        seen = {}
        for i, h in enumerate(hours):
            score += 1 if h > 8 else -1
            if score > 0:
                ans = i + 1
            else:
                seen.setdefault(score, i)
                if score - 1 in seen:
                    ans = max(ans, i - seen[score - 1])
        return ans
```
Notice standard code stores seen.setdefault(score, i) only in else (score <=0), not for positive. The faulty code stores D[curr] for all curr. Does storing positive prefix sums cause any issue? It shouldn't, because D[curr] for positive could later be used as curr-1 for a future current with c = positive+1. For example current c=2, need prefix 1. If D[1] stored earliest prefix 1, good. Standard code also stores positive? Let's check standard: if score >0, ans=i+1, but does not store score. Then later if score becomes 0 and need score-1 = -1, positive scores not needed? Wait for current c<=0, need prefix c-1 <= -1. So positive prefix sums are never needed for c<=0. For current c>0, branch handles. Thus storing positive prefix sums is unnecessary but harmless. But is it harmless? Suppose current c=2, D[2] stored at e1. Later current c=1? Need prefix 0, not 2. No issue. Storing positive values in D doesn't affect D.get(curr-1) for curr<=0 because curr-1 <= -1, so positive entries not queried. For curr>0, D not queried. So harmless.

But wait, if we store D[curr] for all curr, and curr positive, then later if curr becomes negative? D positive remains. No issue.

class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):

            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))

```

Let's maybe compare with a known correct solution:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, hours: List[int]) -> int:
        ans = score = 0
        seen = {}
        for i, h in enumerate(hours):
            score += 1 if h > 8 else -1
            if score > 0:
                ans = i + 1
            else:
                seen.setdefault(score, i)
                if score - 1 in seen:
                    ans = max(ans, i - seen[score - 1])
        return ans
```
Notice in correct solution, `seen.setdefault(score, i)` is in else, so it does NOT store positive scores. The faulty code stores positive scores before branch. Positive keys not used. So no difference. If later score becomes positive again, no query. So no difference.

Now, maybe the bug is not just return ans+1; maybe ans should be initialized to 0 and return ans, but also `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`? Let's search memory: LeetCode 1124 Longest Well-Performing Interval. Common solution:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, hours: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(hours)
        arr = [1 if h > 8 else -1 for h in hours]
        prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(n):
            prefix[i+1] = prefix[i] + arr[i]
        ans = 0
        stack = []
        for i in range(n+1):
            if not stack or prefix[stack[-1]] > prefix[i]:
                stack.append(i)
        for i in range(n, -1, -1):
            while stack and prefix[stack[-1]] < prefix[i]:
                ans = max(ans, i - stack[-1])
                stack.pop()
        return ans
```
Another:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, hours: List[int]) -> int:

                ans = i + 1

                seen.setdefault(score, i)
                if score - 1 in seen:
                    ans = max(ans, i - seen[score - 1])

e2 -1 D[-1]=0 ans=max(1,2-D.get(-2,2)=0)=1
e3 0 D[0]=1 ans=max(1,3-D.get(-1,0)=3)=3
e4 -1 D[-1]=0 ans=max(3,4-D.get(-2,2)=2)=3
e5 0 D[0]=1 ans=max(3,5-D.get(-1,0)=5)=5
Return 5. Correct.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the code should use `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive, D[curr] might be set to e, and later if curr becomes negative and then positive again, D[curr] remains earliest. No issue.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's verify with e=5, previous ans could be 5? A subarray ending at e=4 length 5 (indices 0..4). At e=5, e+1=6 >5. So yes.

Now, let's think about if the code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be after the `ans` update to avoid storing current prefix before checking if it's needed for current ans? For current score <=0, query key score-1, not score. So no. For current score >0, no query. So no.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 not found but there is a prefix less than curr that is not exactly curr-1 due to prefix sums not contiguous? But they are contiguous because step ±1. OK.

Let's maybe think about if the code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive could cause D to contain a key that is equal to curr-1 for a future current curr? Wait future current curr variable could be positive? If future current is positive, branch no query. If future current is <=0, key <= -1. So no. Thus storing positive keys is completely unused. But harmless.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)` because if x is a float, x>8 works. OK.

Now, maybe the code's return `ans + 1` is the only bug. But let's be cautious: Could `ans` be 0 for all negative, return 0. Good. Could `ans` be 1 for [9], return 1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` and then `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`. For curr > 0, D[curr] is set before ans. If curr is positive and D[curr] was previously set, no issue. If curr is positive and new, D[curr]=e. If later curr becomes 0 and query -1, no issue. If later curr becomes 1 and branch, no issue. OK.

Let's think about if the code should include `D[0] = -1` initially? Some solutions do. If we add D[0] = -1, then for curr>0, ans = max(ans, e - D.get(curr-1, e))? Actually with D[0]=-1, for curr>0, D.get(0) = -1, length e - (-1) = e+1, so could unify. But original doesn't. Not needed.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not earlier than current? D only has previous indices, so yes earlier. But if curr-1 equals curr? no.

Now, maybe there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` because for curr > 0, if curr is positive but there is an earlier prefix with sum < curr that gives a longer subarray than e+1? No.

Let's think about if the code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is negative and first occurs at e, and later current curr = curr+1, D.get(curr) returns e. But what if there is an earlier prefix with same curr? D.get returns earliest. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the tuple indexing `(-1, 1)[x > 8]` because in Python, `True` is 1 and `False` is 0. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Looks good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's maybe run a brute-force mental for all sequences of length 4 with code return ans. We did some. Let's do a few more:
Sequence +-++ (1,0,1,2): code return 4. Max? whole sum 3? Wait + - + + = 2? Actually 1-1+1+1=2, positive length4. OK.
Sequence +-+- (1,0,1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.
Sequence +--+ (1,0,-1,0): code return 1. Max 1. OK.
Sequence -++- (-1,0,1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.
Sequence -+-+ (-1,0,-1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.

Sequence +- -? done.

Looks good.

Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's maybe run a brute-force mental for all sequences of length 4 with code return ans. We did some. Let's do a few more:
Sequence +-++ (1,0,1,2): code return 4. Max? whole sum 3? Wait + - + + = 2? Actually 1-1+1+1=2, positive length4. OK.
Sequence +-+- (1,0,1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.
Sequence +--+ (1,0,-1,0): code return 1. Max 1. OK.
Sequence -++- (-1,0,1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.

Looks good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's maybe run a brute-force mental for all sequences of length 4 with code return ans. We did some. Let's do a few more:
Sequence +-++ (1,0,1,2): code return 4. Max? whole sum 3? Wait + - + + = 2? Actually 1-1+1+1=2, positive length4. OK.
Sequence +-+- (1,0,1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.
Sequence +--+ (1,0,-1,0): code return 1. Max 1. OK.

Sequence -+-+ (-1,0,-1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.

Sequence --+- (-1,-2,-1,-2): code return 1. Max 1. OK.

Looks good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's maybe run a brute-force mental for all sequences of length 4 with code return ans. We did some. Let's do a few more:

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's maybe run a brute-force mental for all sequences of length 4 with code return ans. We did some. Let's do a few more:
Sequence +-++ (1,0,1,2): code return 4. Max? whole sum 3? Wait + - + + = 2? Actually 1-1+1+1=2, positive length4. OK.
Sequence +-+- (1,0,1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's maybe run a brute-force mental for all sequences of length 4 with code return ans. We did some. Let's do a few more:

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's maybe run a brute-force mental for all sequences of length 4 with code return ans. We did some. Let's do a few more:
Sequence +-++ (1,0,1,2): code return 4. Max? whole sum 3? Wait + - + + = 2? Actually 1-1+1+1=2, positive length4. OK.
Sequence +-+- (1,0,1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Sequence +-+- (1,0,1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's maybe run a brute-force mental for all sequences of length 4 with code return ans. We did some. Let's do a few more:

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's maybe run a brute-force mental for all sequences of length 4 with code return ans. We did some. Let's do a few more:
Sequence +-++ (1,0,1,2): code return 4. Max? whole sum 3? Wait + - + + = 2? Actually 1-1+1+1=2, positive length4. OK.
Sequence +-+- (1,0,1,0): code return 3. Max 3. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Looks good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's think about if the code should be fixed to:
```
class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
```
This is minimal. But is there any issue with D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) for curr positive causing D to contain positive keys that might be queried if curr-1 is positive? Wait else branch only if curr <=0. So no. But what about if curr > 0 and we use D.get(curr - 1, e) in a modified expression? No.

Let's think about if there is a bug with `D.get(curr - 1, e)` when curr-1 key exists but at an index that is not the earliest because D[curr] = D.get(curr, e) stores earliest only if first occurrence. It does. Good.

Now, maybe the original code's `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` should be `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` but if curr is positive and later used as `curr - 1` for current `curr`? Wait current `curr` in else is <=0, so key <= -1. Positive entries not used. But what about if current `curr` is 0 and D.get(-1) returns an index that was stored when score was -1. Good.

Let's consider if there is a bug with `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` for curr > 0 but if curr > 0 and there is a previous ans larger? We said impossible. Let's prove with e=0, previous ans 0. e=1, previous ans could be 1 (from e=0), e+1=2 >1. e=2, previous ans could be 2 (from e=1), e+1=3. So yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Now, maybe the code should use `D.setdefault(curr, e)` instead of `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)`. Equivalent. No need.

Let's consider if there is a bug with the lambda if A is a generator? `map` consumes generator, enumerate works. OK.

Now, let's think about if the code's return `ans + 1` might be a typo for `return ans` and maybe also `ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))` should be `ans = max(ans, e + 1 if curr > 0 else e - D.get(curr - 1, e))`? The current expression is equivalent because if curr>0, e+1 > ans. But if ans could be larger? no. However, if ans is not initialized to 0? It is. So OK.

Let's test with a case where curr>0 at e=0, ans=1. Return 1. OK.

Now, let's think about if there is a bug with `D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)` for curr positive and later if curr becomes negative, D positive keys remain. But D.get(curr-1) with negative key won't see positive. OK.

Let's maybe run a brute-force mental for all sequences of length 4 with code return ans. We did some. Let's do a few more:

class Solution:
    def longestWPI(self, A):
        curr, ans, D = 0, 0, {}
        for e, i in enumerate(map(lambda x: (-1, 1)[x > 8], A)):
            curr += i
            D[curr] = D.get(curr, e)
            ans = e + 1 if curr > 0 else max(ans, e - D.get(curr - 1, e))
        return ans
